---
title: "An Introduction to Spatial Proteomics: From Raw Images to Spatial Analysis"
description: "From tissue section to single-cell table: what multiplexed protein imaging data look like, the three platform families, the preprocessing and segmentation pipeline, and how the analysis compares with spatial transcriptomics. Every figure uses real public data."
pubDate: '2026-11-23'
lang: en
heroImage: /images/spatial-proteomics-intro/cover.png
localStyles:
  - /styles/spatial-proteomics-intro.css
---

<div class="sp-article not-prose" lang="en"><p class="sp-sub">How multiplexed protein imaging data are shaped, the three families of platforms, the preprocessing pipeline, and how the analysis differs from spatial transcriptomics</p><div class="txt"><p>Spatial proteomics means staining a single tissue section with dozens of antibodies, each "painting" a different protein, imaging it, and then segmenting the image into individual cells. The end product is a quantitative answer to <strong>"how much of each protein is in each cell, and where that cell is"</strong>.</p><p>This post uses real data from SPORA, a public collection hosted by EMBL-EBI <mark>(35 cohorts)</mark>, together with other public repositories such as Zenodo. Following the data format, the imaging platforms, the underlying image structure and the analysis pipeline, it lays out the main path from a tissue section to spatial analysis.</p></div><figure class="txt"><p>The post has seven parts:</p><ol><li>Why keeping spatial coordinates matters.</li><li>Comparing the three platform families: mass spectrometry, cyclic fluorescence, and single-round multispectral imaging.</li><li>Breaking an image down into pixels, channels and bit depth.</li><li>Walking through the pipeline from preprocessing and cell segmentation to annotation.</li><li>File sizes, pyramids, and the four-layer data structure.</li><li>Comparing spatial transcriptomics and spatial proteomics point by point on real data.</li><li>A checklist of the most common mistakes when standardizing data.</li></ol></div><section><div class="part">01</div><h2 id="sp-b4d1f370">Why must we keep the spatial position of cells?</h2><div class="keys"><div class="klab">Key points</div><ul><li>Dissociation-based sequencing loses the spatial relationships between cells in their microenvironment</li><li>Only original coordinates can reconstruct tumor nests and immune infiltration</li><li>Spatial proteomics quantifies protein abundance and cell boundaries directly on the section</li></ul></div><div class="txt"><p>Before encountering <span class="term">multiplexed imaging<span class="gloss">detecting many proteins on one section, simultaneously or over several rounds</span></span>, many researchers know single-cell sequencing better. Conventional single-cell sequencing dissociates tissue into a suspension. You can still count the proportions of each cell type, but once cells leave their microenvironment, <strong>the information about which cells were next to which is lost completely</strong>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 410" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" font-size="13.5" fill="#1f5fbf" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">x, y in µm</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 1 | The basic difference between the two routes: <strong>dissociation sequencing</strong> breaks the tissue into a cell suspension first, so the resulting table has no coordinates; <strong>in situ imaging</strong> reads the signal directly on the section, point by point or field by field, so every cell keeps its own coordinates (schematic).</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>The figure below comes from SPORA (a public data collection hosted by EMBL-EBI; names in parentheses below such as <code>allam2022</code> are its cohort IDs). It shows one lung cancer core from the <code>allam2022</code> cohort's <span class="term">tissue microarray<span class="gloss">dozens of ~1 mm tissue cores stained and imaged on one slide</span></span> (TMA): about 1.5 mm across, 1 µm/pixel, 26 channels, segmented into <mark>17,388 cells</mark>. 2,500 randomly sampled cells are shown:</p><ul><li><strong>Left</strong>: original coordinates discarded and cells scattered randomly in a disk, mimicking the loss of spatial relationships after dissociation.</li><li><strong>Right</strong>: each cell keeps its original coordinates, recovering the real arrangement in the tissue.</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 466" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' cx="270" cy="446" r="7" fill="#1f5fbf"/><text x="282" y="451" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="start">T cell</text><circle cx="390" cy="446" r="7" fill="#2b8a3e"/><text x="402" y="451" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="start">B cell</text><circle cx="510" cy="446" r="7" fill="#c2255c"/><text x="522" y="451" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" text-anchor="start">Stromal</text><circle cx="750" cy="446" r="7" fill="#c4c8cf"/><text x="762" y="451" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="start">Other</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 2 | 2,500 cells from the same IMC lung cancer sample: left, a dissociation-like view with shuffled positions; right, <strong>the real arrangement with in situ coordinates</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Put the two side by side and the difference is obvious:</p><ul><li><strong>Shuffled positions</strong>: epithelial/tumor, T, B, myeloid and stromal cells are simply mixed in the disk in proportion. You cannot tell which cells surround which.</li><li><strong>Real coordinates restored</strong>: on the right, orange epithelial/tumor cells form clear tumor nests, blue T cells gather mostly between the nests, green B cells are scattered, and stromal cells run in between.</li></ul><div class="callout c-key"><span class="clab">Key point</span>For studying the tumor microenvironment and cell–cell interactions, <mark>these original coordinates are the most important information spatial proteomics adds over dissociation-based methods</mark>.</div></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 290" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker id="m1f5fbf" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#2e9e5b"/></marker><marker id="md9822b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#d9822b"/></marker></defs><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/assets_dna_helix.png" x="120" y="20" width="70" height="170" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><text x="155" y="216" font-size="18" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">DNA</text><line x1="210" y1="105" x2="300" y2="105" stroke="#5b6474" stroke-width="3" marker-end="url(#m5b6474)"/><text x="255" y="92" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">Transcription</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/assets_mrna_strand.png" x="320" y="82" width="230" height="46" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><text x="435" y="216" font-size="18" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">RNA</text><line x1="570" y1="105" x2="660" y2="105" stroke="#5b6474" stroke-width="3" marker-end="url(#m5b6474)"/><text x="615" y="92" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">Translation</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/assets_protein_fold.webp" x="690" y="30" width="160" height="150" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><text x="770" y="216" font-size="18" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Protein</text><path d="M330,236 v10 h210 v-10" fill="none" stroke="#d9822b" stroke-width="2"/><text x="435" y="272" font-size="15" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Spatial transcriptomics: reads RNA</text><path d="M690,236 v10 h160 v-10" fill="none" stroke="#1f5fbf" stroke-width="2"/><text x="770" y="272" font-size="15" fill="#1f5fbf" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Spatial proteomics: antibodies → protein</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 3 | Different layers of the same central dogma: spatial transcriptomics reads <strong>RNA</strong>, spatial proteomics uses antibodies to read <strong>protein</strong> (schematic).</figcaption></figure><div class="faq"><div class="fq">Spatial proteomics measures only dozens of proteins, far fewer than transcriptomics. What else can it answer?</div><div class="fa"><p>The two answer questions at different levels:</p><ul><li><strong>Transcriptomics</strong>: good for genome-wide discovery, but sequencing-based single-cell methods lose spatial coordinates after dissociation, and imaging-based spatial transcriptomics detects only RNA.</li><li><strong>Spatial proteomics</strong>: each experiment focuses on <mark>20–60</mark> validated antibody markers (some cyclic platforms reach over a hundred). Directly on FFPE or frozen sections (about 4–5 µm thick), it quantifies the molecules that actually do the work: <strong>protein abundance</strong>. Its clear cell boundaries reveal immune infiltration, tumor compartments and the cellular microenvironment.</li></ul></div></div></section><section><div class="part">02</div><h2 id="sp-33012c67">How do the three imaging platforms get past the color bottleneck?</h2><div class="keys"><div class="klab">Key points</div><ul><li>Spectral overlap limits conventional filters to 4–5 colors per acquisition</li><li>Mass spectrometry, cyclic fluorescence and single-round multispectral imaging each solve this differently</li><li>Choosing a platform trades off channel count, pixel size and field of view</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 306" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker id="m1f5fbf" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#1f5fbf"/></marker><marker id="m6d4bc4" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#6d4bc4"/></marker><marker id="m2e9e5b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#2e9e5b"/></marker><marker id="md9822b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#d9822b"/></marker></defs><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/cards_antibody3.png" x="230" y="10" width="540" height="220" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><line x1="320.0" y1="236" x2="320.0" y2="252" stroke="#6d4bc4" stroke-width="2"/><text x="320.0" y="274" font-size="15" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Metal isotope</text><text x="320.0" y="296" font-size="13.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">→ mass spec imaging</text><line x1="500.0" y1="236" x2="500.0" y2="252" stroke="#1f5fbf" stroke-width="2"/><text x="500.0" y="274" font-size="15" fill="#1f5fbf" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">DNA barcode</text><text x="500.0" y="296" font-size="13.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">→ CODEX etc.</text><line x1="680.0" y1="236" x2="680.0" y2="252" stroke="#d9822b" stroke-width="2"/><text x="680.0" y="274" font-size="15" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Fluorophore</text><text x="680.0" y="296" font-size="13.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">→ cyclic / multispectral</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 4 | The antibody recognizes the protein, and the <strong>tag</strong> on its tail is what the instrument reads. The type of tag determines which platform family is used (schematic).</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>If spatial proteomics needs dozens of antibodies to "paint" a section, why not stain everything at once and image it once on an ordinary fluorescence microscope? The bottleneck is a physical one: <strong>spectral overlap</strong>. A microscope separates signals with color filters, but each fluorescent dye emits over a fairly broad peak, not a thin line.</p><p>Put 40 dyes in at once and their spectra crowd together and bleed into each other, as on the right of the figure below. A conventional filter set can barely separate <mark>about 4–5 colors</mark> at a time. To read dozens or even hundreds of proteins on one section, you have to get around the need to resolve 40 spectra at once, and this has given rise to <strong>three technical routes</strong>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 290" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" stroke="#1a2230" stroke-width="1.5"/><text x="985" y="272" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="end">Wavelength →</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 5 | The color bottleneck: fluorescence emission spectra overlap, so conventional filters can reliably separate only <strong>about 4–5 colors</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Grouped by how they read out the signals carried by dozens of antibodies, mainstream imaging-based spatial proteomics platforms fall into three routes: <strong>mass spectrometry imaging, cyclic fluorescence and single-round multispectral imaging</strong>. The three cards below compare their tagging, readout, channel capacity, pixel size and main cost. These are also what ultimately determine image size and the preprocessing steps later on.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 480" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker text-anchor="start">Pixel ~0.4–1 µm · field ~1 mm across</text><circle cx="30" cy="439" r="4" fill="#6d4bc4"/><text x="42" y="444" font-size="13.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="start">No autofluorescence; slow</text><rect x="340" y="4" width="318" height="470" rx="14" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f5fbf" stroke-width="2"/><rect x="340" y="4" width="318" height="8" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">CODEX · CyCIF</text><text x="499.0" y="90" font-size="13.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">Cell DIVE · MACSima · COMET</text><image y="300" font-size="13.5" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="400" text-anchor="start">Tag: fluorophore (or DNA barcode + probe)</text><circle cx="362" cy="331" r="4" fill="#1f5fbf"/><text x="374" y="336" stroke-width="2"/><rect x="672" y="4" width="318" height="8" rx="4" fill="#d9822b"/><text x="831.0" y="46" font-size="22" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="900" text-anchor="middle">Single-round multispectral</text><text x="831.0" y="72" font-size="13.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">Orion · Vectra (Opal)</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/img_orion.png" x="696" y="100" width="270" height="170" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><circle cx="694" cy="295" r="4" fill="#d9822b"/><text x="706" y="300" font-size="13.5" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="400" text-anchor="start">Tag: many fluorophores on the slide</text><circle cx="694" cy="331" r="4" fill="#d9822b"/><text x="706" y="336" font-size="13.5" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="400" text-anchor="start">Readout: one spectral scan → unmixing</text><circle cx="694" cy="367" r="4" fill="#d9822b"/><text x="706" y="372" font-size="13.5" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="700" text-anchor="start">~6–20 proteins at once</text><circle cx="694" cy="403" r="4" fill="#d9822b"/><text x="706" y="408" font-size="13.5" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="400" text-anchor="start">Pixel ~0.33–0.5 µm · whole slide</text><circle cx="694" cy="439" r="4" fill="#d9822b"/><text x="706" y="444" font-size="13.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="start">Fast, suits large cohorts; limited by overlap</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 6 | The three routes side by side: mass spectrometry imaging, cyclic fluorescence and single-round multispectral imaging compared by <strong>tagging, channel count and resolution</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><ul><li><strong>Mass spectrometry imaging (IMC, MIBI)</strong>: metal isotopes as tags, ablated point by point and read by mass, with no autofluorescence. One run measures about <mark>40</mark> <span class="term">channels<span class="gloss">one grayscale layer, usually one marker</span></span> (mostly proteins, plus DNA dyes and background channels), but it is slow and a single field is usually about 1 mm across.</li><li><strong>Cyclic fluorescence (CODEX, CyCIF, Cell DIVE, MACSima, COMET)</strong>: reads 3–4 proteins per round, then elutes or bleaches and repeats. The total can reach <mark>20–100+ proteins</mark> (depending on the platform) over a whole slide, at the cost of time and the need for <span class="term">per-round registration<span class="gloss">aligning the coordinates of each round</span></span>.</li><li><strong>Single-round multispectral (Orion, Vectra)</strong>: one scan reads fluorescence from about 6–20 proteins, followed by <span class="term">spectral unmixing<span class="gloss">splitting mixed signal using reference spectra</span></span>. Scanning is fast and suits large cohorts, but the channel count is limited.</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 304" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">0.5065 µm/pixel → 99 × 99</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/platforms_chip_imc_mcd.png" x="602" y="20" width="170" height="170" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" style="image-rendering:pixelated"/><rect x="602" y="20" width="170" height="170" fill="none" stroke="#9aa1ad"/><text x="687" y="218" font-size="17" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">IMC</text><text x="687" y="242" font-size="14" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">1 µm/pixel → 50 × 50</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/platforms_chip_mif_imagej.png" x="798" y="20" width="170" height="170" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" style="image-rendering:pixelated"/><rect x="798" y="20" width="170" height="170" fill="none" stroke="#9aa1ad"/><text x="883" y="218" font-size="17" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Author-exported TIFF</text><text x="883" y="242" font-size="14" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">1.517 µm/pixel → 33 × 33</text><line x1="30" y1="272" x2="970" y2="272" stroke="#5b6474" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#m5b6474)"/><text x="14" y="296" font-size="14" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="700" text-anchor="start">Small pixels, more detail</text><text x="986" y="296" font-size="14" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="700" text-anchor="end">Large pixels, less detail</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 7 | The same 50 µm of tissue at different pixel sizes: 294×294 pixels on MACSima, 50×50 on IMC. The last panel is a low-resolution multicolor TIFF the author exported with ImageJ (not an imaging platform), <strong>only 33×33 pixels</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><h3 id="sp-31cfb417">The square cost of pixel size</h3><p>Figure 7 shows the pixel-size trade-off. The same 50 µm square of tissue (about 4–5 cells across) takes 294×294 pixels on MACSima (0.17 µm/pixel), 50×50 on IMC (1 µm/pixel), and only 33×33 in the low-resolution TIFF the author exported with ImageJ (1.517 µm/pixel). Smaller pixels give sharper cell boundaries, but <mark>the number of pixels for the same area grows with the square</mark>.</p><h3 id="sp-1d788092">Choosing a platform</h3><p>Choosing a platform means trading off <strong>channel count, resolution and field of view</strong>:</p><ul><li><strong>Large whole-slide cohorts</strong>: Orion or Vectra are common choices.</li><li><strong>Deep whole-slide profiling of dozens to hundreds of proteins</strong>: cyclic platforms such as CODEX or CyCIF.</li><li><strong>Small TMA fields</strong>: IMC and MIBI are common.</li></ul></div></section><section><div class="part">03</div><h2 id="sp-0269a693">What is a multiplexed image inside a computer?</h2><div class="keys"><div class="klab">Key points</div><ul><li>A multiplexed image is a stack of 2D grayscale matrices of identical size</li><li>Channel count matches the number of markers; bit depth sets intensity precision</li><li>JPG/PNG cannot hold dozens of channels; research uses TIFF-type formats</li></ul></div><div class="txt"><p>The colorful tissue images in software are pseudocolor overlays made for human eyes. In storage, a spatial proteomics image is really <mark>a stack of 2D grayscale images with exactly the same height and width</mark>.</p><p>Figure 8 shows 12 of the 26 channels from the IMC lung cancer core in Section 01, all protein channels: epithelial (E-cadherin, PanKeratin), stromal (Vimentin, SMA), immune (CD3, CD8a, CD4, FoxP3, CD20, CD68, CD163) and proliferation (Ki-67), one image each. Each channel corresponds to one protein, and <strong>brighter means stronger local signal</strong>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><figure><img src="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/img_channel_wall.webp"><figcaption>Figure 8 | Grayscale images of 12 channels from the same IMC lung cancer sample, <strong>each recording the spatial distribution of one protein signal</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Zoom in to the pixel level and a grayscale image is really a <strong>table of numbers</strong>. Take the DNA channel of a real CyCIF file (<code>exemplar-001</code>): in a 4×4 block at the edge of a nucleus, each cell of the grid stores an integer from 0 to 65,535 (from 60,580 inside the nucleus to 36,678 at the edge). The computer records protein signal as numbers: <strong>a larger value means a brighter pixel</strong>, meaning more protein or dye at that spot.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 304" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">= stronger signal here</text><text x="830" y="172" font-size="14" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">DNA dye here; proteins work the same</text><text x="830" y="200" font-size="14" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">Each value 0–65,535 (16-bit)</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 9 | Zoomed to pixels: real 16-bit values in a 4×4 region of the DNA channel. <strong>Larger numbers mean stronger signal</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Two things need to be kept apart here: <strong>channel count</strong> and <span class="term">bit depth<span class="gloss">the number of intensity levels a pixel can take</span></span>. An everyday RGB photo stores only 3 numbers per pixel (red, green, blue), while this CyCIF dataset stores <mark>12 numbers</mark> per pixel, one for each of 12 markers.</p><p>Precision depends on bit depth: 8-bit gives only 0–255 (256 levels), 16-bit gives 0–65,535 (65,536 levels). On the right of the figure below, the same dim region of weak FOXP3 signal keeps <mark>534 distinct values</mark> at 16 bits, but compressed to 8 bits over the full image range only <mark>68</mark> remain, and weak positive detail collapses into coarse banding.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 300" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker id="m1f5fbf" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><rect x="262" y="142" width="82" height="82" fill="#fff" stroke="#9aa1ad"/><rect x="263" y="143" width="80" height="80" fill="#000"/><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/tiff_p1.png" x="263" y="143" width="80" height="80" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><rect x="249" y="149" width="82" height="82" fill="#fff" stroke="#9aa1ad"/><rect x="250" y="150" width="80" height="80" fill="#000"/><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/tiff_p0.png" x="250" y="150" width="80" height="80" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><text x="370" y="262" font-size="15" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">CyCIF file: 12 numbers per pixel</text><text x="370" y="284" font-size="14" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">(12 markers, one grayscale image each)</text><rect x="560" y="50" width="200" height="200" fill="#000"/><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/cards_foxp3_16.png" x="560" y="50" width="200" height="200" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" style="image-rendering:pixelated"/><text x="660" y="276" font-size="15" fill="#2e9e5b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">16-bit: 534 values</text><rect x="780" y="50" width="200" height="200" fill="#000"/><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/cards_foxp3_8.png" x="780" y="50" width="200" height="200" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" style="image-rendering:pixelated"/><text x="880" y="276" font-size="15" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">As 8-bit: 68</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 10 | Left: RGB (3 numbers) vs CyCIF (12 numbers). Right: weak FOXP3 signal <strong>has 534 distinct values at 16 bits and 68 after compression to 8 bits</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>This is why spatial proteomics analysis cannot use everyday image formats such as <code>JPG</code> or <code>PNG</code>. There are three reasons:</p><ul><li>They are designed for RGB display to the human eye and cannot hold dozens of independent channels.</li><li><code>JPG</code> is only 8-bit and uses lossy compression.</li><li>They lack standard fields for channel names and the physical pixel size.</li></ul><div class="callout c-sum"><span class="clab">Summary</span>Research generally uses formats that support multiple pages and metadata: <strong>the TIFF family (e.g. <code>OME-TIFF</code>) or chunked multidimensional array formats (e.g. <code>OME-Zarr</code>)</strong>.</div></div></section><section><div class="part">04</div><h2 id="sp-ca72e646">How does an image become a single-cell table, step by step?</h2><div class="keys"><div class="klab">Key points</div><ul><li>Preprocessing differs by platform; once artifacts are removed, all data join one workflow</li><li>Cell segmentation produces a mask; averaging each channel within it gives the single-cell table</li><li>Over-wide segmentation lets neighbor signal spill over and creates false double positives</li></ul></div><div class="txt"><p>Images straight off the instrument cannot be analyzed directly: <strong>post-acquisition preprocessing depends on how the platform works</strong> (Figure 11):</p><ul><li><strong>Mass spectrometry imaging</strong>: split acquisition regions (ROIs), read metal channels, remove outlier bright spots (hot pixels), compensate isotope spillover, and drop background metal channels.</li><li><strong>Cyclic fluorescence</strong>: correct uneven illumination, stitch small fields of view, register each round to align the section across rounds, and subtract per-round background.</li><li><strong>Single-round multispectral</strong>: centered on spectral unmixing, autofluorescence subtraction, and registration to an <span class="term">H&amp;E<span class="gloss">hematoxylin and eosin, the routine pathology stain</span></span> image of the same section.</li></ul><p>Once these platform-specific noise sources and offsets are removed, all data <mark>join the same downstream analysis path</mark>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 330" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" preprocessing</text><text x="830" y="24" font-size="16" fill="#2e9e5b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Shared by all: the analysis that follows</text><rect x="6" y="44" width="104" height="70" rx="8" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f5fbf" stroke-width="1.6"/><text x="581" y="176" font-size="13" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">Bkg subtraction</text><path d="M644,171 C 700,171 680,182 712,182" fill="none" stroke="#1f5fbf" stroke-width="2.2" marker-end="url(#m1f5fbf)"/><rect x="6" y="228" width="104" height="70" rx="10" fill="#d9822b"/><text x="58" y="270" font-size="15" fill="#fff" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">Multispectral</text><rect x="124" y="234" width="122" height="58" rx="8" fill="#fff" stroke="#d9822b" stroke-width="1.6"/><text x="185" y="268" font-size="13" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">Spectral unmixing</text><rect x="256" y="234" width="122" height="58" rx="8" fill="#fff" stroke="#d9822b" stroke-width="1.6"/><text x="317" y="268" font-size="13" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">Remove autofluor.</text><rect x="388" y="234" width="122" height="58" rx="8" fill="#fff" stroke="#d9822b" stroke-width="1.6"/><text x="449" y="268" font-size="13" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">H&amp;E registration</text><path d="M512,263 C 700,263 680,182 712,182" fill="none" stroke="#d9822b" stroke-width="2.2" marker-end="url(#md9822b)"/><rect x="720" y="50" width="230" height="54" rx="10" fill="#e9f6ee" stroke="#2e9e5b" stroke-width="1.8"/><text x="835" y="83" font-size="15" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">Cell segmentation</text><line x1="835" y1="106" x2="835" y2="118" stroke="#2e9e5b" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#m2e9e5b)"/><rect x="720" y="120" width="230" height="54" rx="10" fill="#e9f6ee" stroke="#2e9e5b" stroke-width="1.8"/><text x="835" y="153" font-size="15" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">Single-cell quantification</text><line x1="835" y1="176" x2="835" y2="188" stroke="#2e9e5b" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#m2e9e5b)"/><rect x="720" y="190" width="230" height="54" rx="10" fill="#e9f6ee" stroke="#2e9e5b" stroke-width="1.8"/><text x="835" y="223" font-size="15" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">Annotation (cluster/gate)</text><line x1="835" y1="246" x2="835" y2="258" stroke="#2e9e5b" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#m2e9e5b)"/><rect x="720" y="260" width="230" height="54" rx="10" fill="#e9f6ee" stroke="#2e9e5b" stroke-width="1.8"/><text x="835" y="293" font-size="15" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">Spatial analysis</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 11 | Each platform handles different physical artifacts during post-acquisition preprocessing, then <strong>all converge on the same segmentation, quantification, annotation and spatial analysis workflow</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>The first step after preprocessing is <span class="term">cell segmentation<span class="gloss">marking the boundary of each cell in the image</span></span>. Common models include <code>Mesmer</code> and <code>Cellpose</code> (pipelines such as <code>steinbock</code> and <code>MCMICRO</code> integrate them). The algorithm combines nuclear stain and membrane protein signals and outputs an integer label image with the same height and width as the original, called a <span class="term">mask<span class="gloss">pixel integers give the cell ID; 0 is background</span></span>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 276" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker id="m1f5fbf" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#1f5fbf"/></marker><marker id="m6d4bc4" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#6d4bc4"/></marker><marker id="m2e9e5b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#2e9e5b"/></marker><marker id="md9822b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#d9822b"/></marker></defs><text x="170" y="26" font-size="15" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">Input: nuclear + membrane channels</text><text x="830" y="26" font-size="15" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="700" text-anchor="middle">Output: mask (one ID per cell)</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/img_seg_network.png" x="120" y="36" width="760" height="230" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /></svg><figcaption>Figure 12 | The segmentation model takes nuclear and membrane channels as input and outputs a <strong>mask</strong> with one ID per cell (schematic: colors stand for IDs; a real mask is an integer matrix with background 0, see below).</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Figure 13 shows a three-panel comparison of a real tile, which makes it easy to check whether the segmentation follows the cell outlines:</p><ul><li><strong>Left</strong>: four-channel overlay.</li><li><strong>Middle</strong>: mask with each cell in a different color.</li><li><strong>Right</strong>: boundaries overlaid on the original image.</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><figure><img src="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/img_segmentation_triptych.png"><figcaption>Figure 13 | Cell segmentation in three panels (a 200 × 200 µm region of the same IMC sample): the four-channel image (left; red = E-cadherin, green = CD3, blue = CD20, yellow = CD68), the color-coded segmentation mask (middle), and the <strong>boundary overlay for quality control</strong> (right).</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>With a mask you can do <span class="term">single-cell quantification<span class="gloss">averaging each channel over a cell's pixels</span></span>. As Figure 14 shows, the program takes every pixel labeled <code>1777</code> in the mask, averages them in each channel (E-cadherin, CD3, CD68 and so on), and that gives <strong>one row of the table</strong> (E-cad 2.04, CD3 2.42, CD68 3.16), together with coordinates and area. IMC pixel values are ion counts, so they are much smaller than the 16-bit grayscale values in the tens of thousands in the CyCIF fluorescence image above. A whole slide often has hundreds of thousands of cells, beyond the 16-bit integer limit of 65,535, so <mark>masks are usually stored as 32-bit integers</mark>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg fill="#efe9fa"/><text x="678" y="128" font-size="15" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">cell</text><text x="678" y="178" font-size="17" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">1777</text><text x="763" y="128" font-size="15" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">E-cad</text><text x="763" y="178" font-size="17" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">2.04</text><text x="848" y="128" font-size="15" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">CD3</text><text x="848" y="178" font-size="17" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">2.42</text><text x="933" y="128" font-size="15" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">CD68</text><text x="933" y="178" font-size="17" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="middle">3.16</text><text x="806" y="236" font-size="15" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">One row of the single-cell table</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 14 | Averaging the pixels of cell 1777 in the mask, channel by channel, gives <strong>one row of the single-cell table</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>With the single-cell table in hand, each cell still needs a <strong>cell type annotation</strong>. The usual approach is unsupervised clustering followed by naming clusters by their highly expressed markers, or the <span class="term">gating<span class="gloss">manually drawing regions on 2D scatter plots to select positive populations</span></span> used in mass cytometry (CyTOF). Figure 15 takes 250 random cells from the full single-cell table of this core and sorts them by their top marker. Epithelial (E-cadherin/PanKeratin), stromal (Vimentin/SMA), T cell (CD3/CD8a/CD4), B cell (CD20), macrophage (CD68/CD163) and proliferating (Ki-67) cells appear one block after another.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><figure><img src="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/img_cell_table_heatmap.png"><figcaption>Figure 15 | Heatmap of 12 markers in 250 random cells (sorted by each cell's top marker): <strong>distinct cell populations form clear blocks</strong>.<span class="cbar"><span>0</span><i style="background:linear-gradient(90deg,#f7fbff,#c6dbef,#6baed6,#2171b5,#08306b)"></i><span>99th percentile of this marker</span></span></figcaption></figure><div class="faq"><div class="fq">If segmentation is inaccurate, how much does it affect downstream analysis?</div><div class="fa"><p>The single-cell table averages the pixels inside each mask boundary, so if a boundary takes in a few extra pixels from a neighbor, that neighbor's strong signal <strong>"spills over"</strong> into it. For example, when a T cell sits right against a tumor cell, an over-wide segmentation gives the T cell both high CD3 and high E-cadherin, creating a <mark>false "double positive"</mark>.</p><div class="callout c-warn"><span class="clab">Note</span>Different segmentation models also disagree on cell size and cell count. After segmentation, overlay the boundaries on the original image and spot-check them, and filter out abnormal cells by <strong>cell area and DNA intensity</strong>.</div></div></div></section><section><div class="part">05</div><h2 id="sp-65c7bcac">Why is the data so large? Four layers and metadata</h2><div class="keys"><div class="klab">Key points</div><ul><li>Single images range from tens of MB to tens of GB; whole slides take most of the storage</li><li>Pyramids and tiling let you read a 100 GB image one field of view at a time</li><li>A sample contains images, masks, tables and metadata; metadata is the most error-prone</li></ul></div><div class="txt"><p>Spatial proteomics <strong>data sizes vary enormously</strong> (Figure 16):</p><ul><li><strong>Small-field samples</strong>: an IMC core is only about 20 MB (<code>allam2022</code>) to 40 MB (<code>cords2024</code>); a MIBI field (<code>greenwald2026</code>) is about 170 MB.</li><li><strong>Resampled large tissue</strong>: a CODEX intestine section (<code>hickey2023</code>) resampled to 1 µm/pixel is about 0.6 GB.</li><li><strong>Whole-slide images</strong>: covering 1–2 cm of tissue at 0.3–0.5 µm/pixel gives about 2 billion pixels per channel. CODEX liver cancer whole slides (<code>ruf2023</code>) average about 35 GB, and Orion whole slides (<code>lin2023</code>) about 50 GB.</li></ul><p>Of SPORA's 4.9 TB in total, <mark>just 4 whole-slide cohorts account for 73%</mark>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 334" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker id="m1f5fbf" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#1f5fbf"/></marker><marker id="m6d4bc4" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#6d4bc4"/></marker><marker id="m2e9e5b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#2e9e5b"/></marker><marker id="md9822b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#d9822b"/></marker></defs><line x1="330.0" y1="14" x2="330.0" y2="300" stroke="#e3e6eb"/><text x="330.0" y="322" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">10 MB</text><line x1="485.0" y1="14" x2="485.0" y2="300" stroke="#e3e6eb"/><text x="485.0" y="322" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">100 MB</text><line x1="640.0" y1="14" x2="640.0" y2="300" stroke="#e3e6eb"/><text x="640.0" y="322" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">1 GB</text><line x1="795.0" y1="14" x2="795.0" y2="300" stroke="#e3e6eb"/><text x="795.0" y="322" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">10 GB</text><line x1="950.0" y1="14" x2="950.0" y2="300" stroke="#e3e6eb"/><text x="950.0" y="322" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">100 GB</text><text x="320" y="48" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="end">IMC tissue microarray core (allam2022)</text><rect x="330" y="30" width="46.7" height="28" rx="4" fill="#6d4bc4" fill-opacity=".85"/><text x="384.6596493279171" y="49" font-size="14" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="start">~20 MB</text><text x="320" y="94" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="end">IMC core (cords2024, mean)</text><rect x="330" y="76" width="93.3" height="28" rx="4" fill="#6d4bc4" fill-opacity=".85"/><text x="431.3192986558342" y="95" font-size="14" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="start">~40 MB</text><text x="320" y="140" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="end">MIBI field (greenwald2026, mean)</text><rect x="330" y="122" width="190.7" height="28" rx="4" fill="#6d4bc4" fill-opacity=".85"/><text x="528.7195828136324" y="141" font-size="14" fill="#6d4bc4" font-weight="800" text-anchor="start">~170 MB</text><text x="320" y="186" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="end">CODEX intestine section, 1 µm (hickey2023)</text><rect x="330" y="168" width="275.6" height="28" rx="4" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity=".85"/><text x="613.6134438094648" y="187" font-size="14" fill="#1f5fbf" font-weight="800" text-anchor="start">~0.6 GB</text><text x="320" y="232" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="end">CODEX liver cancer whole slide (ruf2023, mean)</text><rect x="330" y="214" width="549.3" height="28" rx="4" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity=".85"/><text x="887.3305468742927" y="233" font-size="14" fill="#1f5fbf" font-weight="800" text-anchor="start">~35 GB</text><text x="320" y="278" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230" font-weight="500" text-anchor="end">Orion whole slide (lin2023, mean)</text><rect x="330" y="260" width="573.3" height="28" rx="4" fill="#d9822b" fill-opacity=".85"/><text x="911.3403506720829" y="279" font-size="14" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="800" text-anchor="start">~50 GB</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 16 | Measured size of single images (log scale): small fields are tens to a hundred-odd MB, <strong>whole slides often reach tens of GB</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>At 20–60 channels and 16 bits, an uncompressed whole-slide image can reach <mark>about 170 GB</mark>, far too large to load into the memory of an ordinary computer. For smooth viewing and computation, mainstream formats use <span class="term">pyramid<span class="gloss">multiple copies of the image at progressively lower resolution</span></span> and <span class="term">tiling<span class="gloss">each level cut into fixed-size tiles compressed separately</span></span> structures.</p><p>As Figure 17 shows, when you view the whole slide the software reads only the smallest thumbnail level, and when you zoom in it reads only the few tiles that fall in the field of view. Both <code>OME-TIFF</code> and <code>OME-Zarr</code>, which suits on-demand loading from the cloud, rely on this <strong>"read only what you need" mechanism</strong>.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 312" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" y="188" font-size="15" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">Cannot load into memory</text><rect x="640" y="60" width="0" height="0"/></svg><figcaption>Figure 17 | Pyramids and tiling: browsing reads the smallest level, zooming in reads only the tiles hit, <strong>avoiding loading an image of 100+ GB</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>A complete spatial proteomics sample is not just images. As Figure 18 shows, it consists of <strong>four layers</strong>:</p><ol><li><strong>Multichannel images (<code>images</code>)</strong></li><li><strong>Segmentation masks (<code>labels</code>)</strong></li><li><strong>Single-cell table (<code>tables</code>)</strong>: e.g. <span class="term"><code>.h5ad</code><span class="gloss">an HDF5-based AnnData single-cell table file</span></span>.</li><li><strong><span class="term">Metadata<span class="gloss">the "manual" describing sample and image properties</span></span></strong>: records each channel's antibody and target protein UniProt ID, physical pixel size, sample information, and registration to H&amp;E.</li></ol><p>Modern container formats such as <code>SpatialData</code> are built on <code>OME-Zarr</code> and use <code>images</code>, <code>labels</code>, <code>tables</code> and <span class="term"><code>attrs</code><span class="gloss">global attributes and metadata fields</span></span> to put the four layers into one folder with a <strong>shared coordinate system</strong>, so images, masks and tables can be queried together.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><figure>
    <svg viewBox="0 0 1000 330" width="100%">
      <g font-size="14">

          <rect x="40" y="0" width="150" height="150" fill="#5b2a2a" stroke="#1a2230"/><rect x="28" y="12" width="150" height="150" fill="#2a5b33" stroke="#1a2230"/><rect x="16" y="24" width="150" height="150" fill="#2a3e5b" stroke="#1a2230"/><rect x="4" y="36" width="150" height="150" fill="#1a2230" stroke="#fff"/>
          <text x="79" y="120" fill="#fff" text-anchor="middle" font-size="13">c × y × x</text>

          <text x="100" y="169" text-anchor="middle" font-weight="700" fill="#a16207">Single-cell table · tables</text>

        <text x="500" y="300" text-anchor="middle" font-size="14" fill="#1a2230">SpatialData puts the four layers in one container (images · labels · tables · attrs) with a shared coordinate system (incl. the transform to H&amp;E)</text>

    <figcaption>Figure 18 | The four layers of a sample: images, segmentation masks, single-cell table and metadata, <strong>which SpatialData can package together</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>When analyzing public data, <mark>the most common problem is usually not the pixel matrix but metadata that is missing or easy to misread</mark>. Figure 19 compares three real cases:</p><ul><li><strong>Complete file</strong>: correct channel names and pixel size.</li><li><strong>Unnamed channels</strong>: some public MIBI files have all 20 channels unnamed (the pixel size is there: 0.39 µm). You can only match them to the antibody table in the paper, and one row off means every channel is mislabeled.</li><li><strong>Unusual units</strong>: a CODEX file records pixel size in centimeters (5.065E-5 cm, i.e. 0.5065 µm). This is legal in the OME standard, but software that ignores the unit and assumes micrometers will be off by a factor of 10,000.</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 266" cx="874" cy="96" r="5" fill="#fff" stroke="#8a6d3b" stroke-width="1.5"/><text x="923" y="116" font-size="12.5" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">Channel names:</text><text x="923" font-weight="600" text-anchor="middle">In cm: 5.065E-5 cm = 0.5065 µm</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 19 | Three real states of a file: <strong>complete manual (left), missing channel names (middle), and pixel size recorded in centimeters, easy to misread (right)</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>So when you get a file from a public download or straight off the instrument, do a round of <strong>metadata checks</strong>:</p><ol><li>Check channel order and protein names against the paper's methods section, and flag and exclude antibody-free background metal channels such as <code>ArAr80</code> and <code>Xe131</code>.</li><li>Check the pixel size value and its unit.</li><li>Confirm that the bit depth has not been silently reduced, and whether multiple ROIs or TMA cores have been split into separate samples.</li></ol><div class="callout c-key"><span class="clab">Key point</span>Only when the "manual" is accurate <mark>does downstream quantitative analysis hold up</mark>.</div></div></section><section><div class="part">06</div><h2 id="sp-f1e2313e">How do spatial proteomics and spatial transcriptomics compare?</h2><div class="keys"><div class="klab">Key points</div><ul><li>They start from opposite data shapes and sparsity; preprocessing and normalization must differ</li><li>Proteomics has few, dense features and often skips PCA for direct clustering or gating</li><li>After annotation both are stored with coordinates, and spatial tools are fully shared</li></ul></div><div class="txt"><p>Researchers with spatial transcriptomics experience often ask whether their existing pipeline carries over. The answer is: <mark>the second half is shared; the first half must be handled separately</mark>. Below, the same pipeline is run on a Xenium whole-slide breast cancer sample, an imaging-based spatial transcriptomics method (<code>GSM9509156</code>, 69,009 cells × 498 genes), and on the SPORA <code>allam2022</code> IMC lung cancer cohort (26 TMA cores, 187,028 cells in total).</p><h3 id="sp-aa52bbfe">Difference 1: the shape of the raw data</h3><p>The first difference is in the <strong>raw form</strong> (Figure 20):</p><ul><li><strong>Xenium</strong>: produces coordinates of RNA molecules labeled with gene names, which must be assigned to cells. (Sequencing-based Visium has capture spots that cover several cells, so it also needs <span class="term">deconvolution<span class="gloss">estimating the proportions of cells within one spot</span></span>.)</li><li><strong>IMC</strong>: continuous grayscale pixel images; cell segmentation must draw the boundaries before averaging.</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 0 1000 486" width="100%" font-family='-apple-system,"PingFang SC","Noto Sans CJK SC",sans-serif' class=""><defs><marker id="m5b6474" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#5b6474"/></marker><marker id="m1f5fbf" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#1f5fbf"/></marker><marker id="m6d4bc4" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#6d4bc4"/></marker><marker id="m2e9e5b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#2e9e5b"/></marker><marker id="md9822b" markerWidth="10" markerHeight="10" refX="8" refY="5" orient="auto"><path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#d9822b"/></marker></defs><text x="250" y="28" font-size="18" fill="#d9822b" font-weight="900" text-anchor="middle">Spatial transcriptomics (Xenium)</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/cards_xen_left.webp" x="40" y="44" width="420" height="432" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /><text x="750" y="28" font-size="18" fill="#1f5fbf" font-weight="900" text-anchor="middle">Spatial proteomics (IMC)</text><image href="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/img_imc_zoom.webp" x="540" y="44" width="432" height="432" preserveAspectRatio="xMidYMid meet" /></svg><figcaption>Figure 20 | Different starting points: left, Xenium's <strong>discrete RNA molecule coordinates</strong> (the gray background is DAPI nuclear stain; field about 150 µm across); right, IMC's continuous multichannel pixels and segmentation boundaries.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Different starting data lead directly to single-cell tables with <strong>opposite sparsity</strong>. Figure 21 takes 8 cells × 10 features from each of two real samples:</p><ul><li><strong>Xenium</strong>: stores integer counts of RNA molecules captured in each cell. The median cell has only 88 molecules and 41 genes, and <mark>91.6% of the full table is 0</mark>, so it suits a sparse matrix.</li><li><strong>IMC</strong>: stores the mean ion intensity within each cell boundary. The background itself carries weak signal, so only <mark>0.7% of the full table is 0</mark>; it is a dense, continuous matrix with almost no zeros.</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><svg viewBox="0 stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="540" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.94" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="568" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.52" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="596" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="1.00" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="624" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.49" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="652" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.65" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="680" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.49" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="708" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.63" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="736" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.84" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="764" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="1.00" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><rect x="792" y="240" width="28" height="28" fill="#1f5fbf" fill-opacity="0.99" stroke="#e3e0f2"/><text x="680" y="302" font-size="18" fill="#1f5fbf" font-weight="900" text-anchor="middle">Only 0.7% of the full table is 0</text><text x="680" y="326" font-size="13" fill="#5b6474" font-weight="400" text-anchor="middle">Shown: 8 real cells × 10 features</text></svg><figcaption>Figure 21 | 8 real cells × 10 features from each (the zero fraction of a patch is not that of the whole table): the full Xenium table is <strong>91.6% zeros</strong> (integer counts); the full IMC table is only 0.7% zeros (continuous intensities).</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Because the value distributions differ, <span class="term">normalization<span class="gloss">removing scale differences and extreme values to make data comparable</span></span> must also be handled differently (Figure 22):</p><ul><li><strong>Transcriptomics</strong>: in Xenium, <code>EPCAM</code> is 0 in 77% of cells. The usual practice is to <strong>scale by each cell's total molecule count, then apply a <code>log1p</code> transform</strong> (i.e. ln(x+1)).</li><li><strong>Proteomics</strong>: only a few dozen antibodies are measured and total signal differs greatly between cell types, so you cannot scale by the total. Instead, <code>arcsinh</code> (inverse hyperbolic sine; log-like in shape but defined at 0, usually applied after dividing by a cofactor) is used to compress the long high tail, and each marker is then clipped at its 99th percentile and scaled to 0–1, so that a few extremely high cells do not push most cells toward 0.</li></ul></div><figure class="bleed"><figure><img src="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/compare_dist.png"><figcaption>Figure 22 | Distributions and normalization: transcriptomics uses total-count scaling plus log1p; <strong>proteomics uses arcsinh plus 99th-percentile clipping</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Dimensionality also affects dimension reduction and clustering. Figure 23 runs the same <span class="term">PCA<span class="gloss">principal component analysis, compressing high-dimensional features first</span></span> and <span class="term">UMAP<span class="gloss">a dimension reduction method that places cells on a 2D plane</span></span> workflow on quality-controlled Xenium data (cells with fewer than 10 molecules removed, leaving 68,196 cells × 498 genes) and on subsampled IMC data (the 18 proteins measured in all 26 cores, 60,000 random cells). With 498 genes the cell populations separate very cleanly. With 18 proteins there is less information, the clusters run into each other, and the "other" fraction is higher.</p><p>That is why <strong>proteomics often skips PCA and clusters or gates directly on the markers</strong>. And if the panel has no endothelial marker, there is no way to find an endothelial population.</p></div><figure class="bleed"><figure><img src="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/compare_types_legend.png" style="max-width:640px;margin:0 auto 4px"><img src="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/compare_umap.webp"><figcaption>Figure 23 | UMAP comparison: Xenium (498 genes) has well-separated clusters, <strong>IMC (18 proteins) clusters sit closer together</strong>. This coarse clustering of Xenium merged B cells into the lymphocyte group, and the IMC panel has no endothelial marker, so each side is missing one class.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><p>Once cells are annotated and placed back at their in situ coordinates, <strong>spatial analysis methods are fully shared between the two data types</strong>. Take <span class="term">neighborhood enrichment<span class="gloss">comparing how often each cell type occurs among neighbors versus after random shuffling</span></span> as an example: take the 10 nearest neighbors of each cell, count them, and compute a z-score (positive/red means a tendency to cluster, negative/blue means mutual avoidance).</p><p>As Figure 24 shows, although the tissues differ, the pattern of results is strikingly similar: positive values on the diagonal show same-type cells clustering, and tumor and T cells show strongly negative values on both sides (Xenium −79, IMC −90; strongly negative in both the row and column direction).</p></div><figure class="bleed"><figure><img src="/images/spatial-proteomics-intro/compare_nhood.png"><figcaption>Figure 24 | Neighborhood enrichment z-score heatmaps (color scale clipped at ±20): both show same-type clustering, and <strong>tumor and T cells are neighbors far less often than by chance (−79 / −90)</strong>.</figcaption></figure><div class="txt"><div class="callout c-sum"><span class="clab">Summary</span>Spatial transcriptomics has many features, sparse values, and starts from molecule points; spatial proteomics has few features, continuous values, and starts from multichannel images. As long as each gets its own preprocessing, segmentation and normalization, and the single-cell table and coordinates are saved as <code>AnnData</code> (or as the table layer in a <code>SpatialData</code> container), <mark>downstream clustering and spatial statistics can share one set of tools</mark>.</div></div></section><section><div class="part">07</div><h2 id="sp-7686e39c">Run through a checklist before you start analyzing</h2><div class="keys"><div class="klab">Key points</div><ul><li>Standardization errors mostly come from metadata and precision checks</li><li>Before ingestion, check channel mapping, background removal and pixel units one by one</li><li>Keep the matrix, the pipeline and the metadata in view and you avoid most detours</li></ul></div><div class="txt"><p>When organizing spatial proteomics data from many sources into a standard container (for example into <code>SpatialData</code>), <mark>errors usually come not from the algorithms but from details that were not checked</mark>. The table below lists the <strong>five most common pitfalls and how to check for each</strong>.</p></div><table class="pit"><tr><td><strong>Misordered channels</strong> or inconsistent protein names</td><td>E.g. PanCK and Pan-Keratin used interchangeably, or mass spec export files ordered by metal isotope mass rather than antibody name; <strong>map each to a UniProt ID using the metadata</strong></td></tr><tr><td><strong>Non-protein background or blank channels</strong> mixed into the table</td><td>E.g. IMC argon/xenon background channels <code>ArAr80</code> and <code>Xe131</code>, or blank background rounds in CyCIF; mark them as non-protein channels and <strong>remove them before single-cell quantification</strong></td></tr><tr><td><strong>Missing pixel size</strong> or units easy to misread</td><td>E.g. no µm information, or a value recorded in cm (5.065E-5 cm = 0.5065 µm) that software reads as µm; <strong>go back to the raw file or the paper and record the source</strong></td></tr><tr><td><strong>Reduced numeric precision</strong> or accidentally changed data type</td><td>E.g. a 16-bit image compressed to 8 bits, or integers (counts, mask IDs) saved as floating point <code>float64</code>; <strong>compare value ranges and types against the source file value by value</strong></td></tr><tr><td><strong>Several samples in one file</strong> or mismatched mask IDs</td><td>E.g. raw IMC <code>.mcd</code> files often bundle several acquisition regions (ROIs) and laser test areas; split them by region, and check that mask IDs and the cell IDs in the single-cell table (<code>cell_id</code>) <strong>match one to one</strong></td></tr></table><div class="txt"><p>Whether you are reusing a large public collection like SPORA or analyzing fresh sections from your own lab, at the start you do not need to memorize byte-level format details. <strong>Keep these three core ideas in mind</strong> and you will avoid most detours when reading images, choosing a platform and running the analysis.</p></div><div class="take"><div><span>1</span><b>An image is a table of numbers</b><em>Channel count, bit depth and resolution set the storage size. Know the shape of the matrix before drawing conclusions.</em></div><div><span>2</span><b>Different preprocessing, shared downstream</b><em>Once the three platform families remove their physical artifacts, segmentation through spatial statistics can share tools with transcriptomics.</em></div><div><span>3</span><b>Check metadata before analysis</b><em>If channel mapping, pixel units, bit depth or mask IDs don't match, every later calculation is distorted.</em></div></div></section></div>
